ReactiveDynamics.jl  ·  ReactiveDynamics.jl — the agentic layer

ReactiveDynamics.jl Modeling Copilot

A guided workflow for decisions whose answer depends on how an operation behaves over time: shared capacity, lead times, feedback, decisions taken mid-course, the dynamics a spreadsheet flattens.

Describe the operation in conversation. The copilot builds a high-fidelity model beneath it, tries to break it, and uses it to compare the options and explain what drives the answer. Decisions of this kind — a launch-capacity reservation, a bid under a staffing ceiling, a portfolio whose funding follows its own results, a service backlog that feeds back into demand — today need a modeler, weeks of work, and trust in a workbook nobody else can read. The copilot turns that into a guided session with the person who owns the decision, with each step checked by the engine rather than by the reader's faith. The analyst stays at the level of the decision, in conversation, and the model follows from what they say; every number says what it rests on and replays exactly from its seed. This page sets out the vision, what exists today, and the direction.
what it is a reusable modeling capability: an interview, a running model of how your operation behaves, a health pass that can refuse, and a briefing that ships as a generated report — run in conversation
who drives it an analyst or a strategy lead, in business language. Today it runs in Claude Code on a checkout of the repository with Julia installed, so someone technical sets it up; the direction is a tool any assistant can call (see §5)
why it can be trusted the model is a checked data document in a closed vocabulary, never code — validated before it runs, reproducible from a seed afterwards
the case on this page a launch-supply decision in pharma, captured with AI on both sides: the operator was a role-played agent answering from a fact sheet sealed before the first message. The company is invented; the numbers are real runs of the model, on paired single-seed paths, not ensembles

The expensive part of a decision like this is not the arithmetic. It is getting the question right, getting the operation right, and knowing which parts of the answer you are entitled to defend in the room. That is why these questions go to a specialist, take weeks, and come back as a workbook the decision-maker has to take on faith.

The copilot does that work with the decision owner. It interviews them about the decision, turns their description of the operation into a running model, stress-tests the model against what they said the business does, and only then reports a number — with the mechanism that produced it and an honest account of the evidence behind it. The same capability serves the next question and the one after: a way of working, not a one-off model.

1 · More decisions get a real model, and the owner stays in the loop

The threshold for a real model drops. Today a question about how an operation behaves — who waits when the line is full, what a reservation is worth before the readout, what happens to intake when the backlog grows — gets a model only when it is big enough to justify a specialist. The rest get a workbook. A workbook can hold a monthly time series and even a Monte Carlo draw, but it cannot easily hold one shared line allocated month by month between products whose launch dates are themselves random, plus a rule that changes the plan when a readout lands. Building that in a spreadsheet means writing a simulator inside it that nobody can check. In the case on this page, a sheet built on average dates has no path on which a late qualification delays a filing, so it could not have shown the $72M loss on one path that made the operator change the plan.

The owner stays in the loop. The interview runs in business language with the person who owns the decision. Every assumption lands in a written brief they agree to, including which inputs were their own estimates and what they expect the answer to be, so the result is compared against the expectation rather than quietly replacing it.

The answer is defensible, not just plausible. Each number arrives with the evidence behind it, the command that produced it and the seed that replays it. When the CFO asks "what if the second line costs more", the answer is one edited input and a batch of re-runs the same day, not a rebuilt workbook.

It compounds. A study is a directory — brief, model, health report, briefing, every command — and it ships as a generated report the modeler can forward as is. The next question about the same operation starts from that model.

2 · The engine models more than queues; the copilot's checks are catching up

The engine underneath is a general process simulator. Contention for shared capacity is the pattern the first studies exercised, and it is where the copilot's checks are sharpest. It is one of several patterns a model can carry, often in the same model:

PatternBusiness exampleCopilot today
Contentiontwo launches on one fill line; a bench of scientistsauthored and checked end to end
Lead times and clockstech transfer, a build, a regulatory reviewauthored and checked
Feedbacka backlog that throttles intake; share lost after a stock-out; funding that follows resultsauthored and run — the case model has 12 rates that read the live state — but no check yet tests loop behaviour
Decisions taken mid-coursereview a contract at a readout; an allocation policyauthored and run; the case turned on one
Entities with a lifecycleprojects with attributes moving through phasesauthored and run
Uncertaintyreadout odds, lead-time rangesauthored and run; options compared on the same random draws
Coupled modelsa supply model feeding a commercial model; several sitesin the engine; not yet expressible as a copilot model document

The interview's qualifying test and the plausibility checks were written for contention. Widening them to feedback and coupled models is part of the direction in §5 (W25).

3 · Four steps, and a check that can stop the answer
StepWhat happensWhat you getGate
Framean interview: the decision, the real alternative, what is shared, what takes time, what reacts to what, what counts as an answera written brief, agreed before anything is builtthe question needs a model, not arithmetic
Authorthe operation becomes a running model, one version per optiona checked model document per optionthe validator returns no errors
Diagnoseplausibility checks ask whether the model does what you said the business doesa health report, every failure stateda failed check blocks the number until it is fixed, or explained in writing and accepted by the owner
Reportevery option is run on the same random outcomes; differences are read against their own noisea briefing: estimate, mechanism, and what it rests onevery number carries its evidence and a seed

If the question is arithmetic, the interview says so and stops. Nothing shared, nothing delayed, nothing reacting: a spreadsheet is the right tool. Numbers the owner is unsure of are recorded as estimates, and the published audit of each captured session says which of them were tested.

A failed check is not a footnote. In the case, every option failed the same check, that every step of the model fires at least once in a run. That was by design, not a defect: one network carries every option and every readout outcome, so any single run leaves the steps of the paths it did not take idle, such as a second line nobody built, or Tern's production when Tern's trial fails. The study reported a number only after DOCTOR.md showed that each idle step fires in the runs where its path is taken, and a flag that lets an option declare its intentionally idle steps is on the tracker (W20). A horizon warning also stands: both products are still ramping at end-2032, so every value is a within-horizon number, and the report says so.

TermMeaning on this page
option / armone version of the model per choice on the table, identical except for what the choice changes
seedthe number that fixes every random draw, so a run replays exactly
paired pathsthe options run on the same seeds, so they see the same readout dates and lead times, and the difference is the option, not the luck
ensemblemany seeds of one option, summarised with a spread; the stronger form of evidence, not used in this case
checka plausibility test on a running model, such as whether a bottleneck really binds
4 · The number can be trusted because the model is data, not code

The operation ends up as a structured document in a closed vocabulary the engine understands — places that hold resources, transitions that consume them over time, rates, rules, costs, what each option changes. Nothing in the document is a program, and nothing in it can name one. That is what makes the rest possible:

  • Checked before it runs. A pure validator reads the document and returns every problem with its exact location. A malformed model produces diagnostics, not a plausible wrong number. That is what makes it safe to put a language model in the authoring seat: it proposes, a deterministic checker disposes, and the loop repeats until the checker is silent.
  • Checked again, for meaning. A schema cannot tell you that your bottleneck never binds or that the horizon cuts off the payoff. The plausibility checks do.
  • Reproducible by construction. A run is fixed by the model and a seed; options are compared on the same random outcomes.
  • Guarded in CI. The reference the copilot reads is generated from the engine and fails the build if it drifts, and a known-answer test re-derives a published result through the same path on every change.

Where the guarantee stops today. It covers the model, not all the arithmetic around it. In the case, the documents were emitted by a short generator script, each then validated as data; and the odds-weighting behind the headline ran in analysis scripts the assistant wrote during the session. Those scripts are published with the study and were re-run independently over the stored runs, and every figure matched — but they are code, reviewed by reading. Moving that weighting into the engine's own effect command is part of the direction (W27).

Contrast with a code-writing assistant. An assistant using a general simulation library writes the model as code, and code cannot be checked before it runs: it crashes or returns something, and something is the dangerous case. Here the machine can author the model, be wrong, be told exactly where, and try again.

5 · Inside the copilot: who does what

The copilot is a thin layer of procedure over an open-source engine. Skills carry the procedure for each phase: what to ask, what to check, how to report. A single command-line tool, rdx, does the work: validate, run, diagnose, run ensembles, measure the effect of an option, draw the network. Underneath is ReactiveDynamics.jl, a stochastic process engine built on AlgebraicAgents.jl.

The division follows what each kind of component is good for. A skill is procedure the assistant loads only when the phase needs it, so the conversation carries one phase's rules at a time. A subagent buys a clean context: the author's repair loop produces pages of diagnostics nobody needs to see, and a critic is only useful if it has not read the author's reasoning. An MCP server buys reach, the same verbs for a host with no terminal, and a warm Julia session. A hook buys enforcement a prompt cannot promise, because it runs whether or not the assistant remembers to.

Every component, its role and what it works with:

ComponentKindWhat it doesWorks withStatus
rd-studyskill, orchestratorreads the study directory, resumes at the phase it implies, enforces the gatesthe user; the four phase skillsshipped
rd-frameskillinterviews the owner, runs the qualifying arithmetic, pre-registers what would change the answer; writes BRIEF.mdthe usershipped
rd-authorskillturns the brief into one model document per option and repairs it until validate is silentrdx validate, rdx run; the generated schema referenceshipped
rd-doctorskillruns the nine plausibility checks and writes DOCTOR.md; a failure goes back to AUTHORrdx diagnoseshipped
rd-reportskillmeasures the options on paired paths or ensembles and writes FINDINGS.md, then the generated REPORT.htmlrdx effect, rdx ensemble, rdx draw; render_report.jlshipped
rdxCLIthe six verbs over the engine; JSON out, exit 0 ok / 1 findings / 2 errorReactiveDynamics.jlshipped
knowledgefilesthe normative spec and ADRs, cited by section; the schema reference, generated from the engine and drift-gated in CIevery skill reads it when neededshipped
rd-modelersubagentruns rd-author's procedure in its own context, so the repair loop's noise stays out of the conversation, and hands back only the validated modelrdx, through Bash or MCPW9
rd-criticsubagentfresh-context review: the model against the brief, the briefing's prose against its numbersthe study's files, read-onlyW9
rdMCP serverthe six verbs as tools (rd_validate, rd_run, …) and the spec as resources, over a warm sessionany MCP hostW8
validate hookhookvalidates every write to a *.rdj.json and returns the diagnostics to the assistantrdx validateW10
pluginpackaging/rd:study and the phase commands, one-step installClaude CodeW12

Section 8 shows the other half of the stack: what the engine receives from all this, traced from the conversation to the model document.

6 · What exists today, and where it is going

Today. The engine, the four-phase workflow as five skills, the rdx tool, the generated schema reference with its drift gate, the known-answer test, the generated study report, and four published studies, three with their captured sessions. A study runs in Claude Code on a checkout of the repository: the rd-study skill runs the interview, writes the brief, authors the model, runs the checks and asks for sign-off at each gate, and every command and its result is visible in the conversation.

The direction, in the order it retires risk — first what makes the number more trustworthy, then what widens who can reach it:

DirectionWhat it changesTracker
Fast ensemblesan option-level ensemble in minutes rather than 53 minutes, so studies stop falling back to paired pathsW24
Declared dead stepsan option declares what it intentionally never fires, so a failed check means a bugW20
Weighting in the engineforced outcomes and odds-weighting inside rdx effect, not in session scriptsW27
A critica separate agent audits the briefing's prose against the numbers — the class of error this case retracted out loudW9
Beyond contentiona qualifying test and checks for feedback and mid-course decisions; coupled models as documentsW25
Validate hookevery edit to a model document validated automaticallyW10
MCP serverthe same operations as tools any assistant can call, with no terminalW8
Plugin and study cardone-step install; a one-page summary over the generated reportW12 · W11

None of this changes what a study is. The first half makes the answer harder to get wrong; the second half moves the copilot from something a developer sets up to something an analyst opens.

7 · The case: do we pay for capacity before the readouts?

The question. A VP of launch readiness has two late-stage biologics reading out nine months apart (Kestrel, 60% odds; Tern, 45%), one sterile fill-finish line that can carry one launch but maybe not two, a contract-manufacturer (CMO) offer that expires on 30 November 2026, and a CFO who says not to pay for capacity on a 45% asset. Reserve the CMO slot now, build a second line, or wait? The operator arrived leaning toward a reservation sized for Tern.

How it was captured. Both sides were AI. The operator was a role-played agent that wrote its facts to a sheet before the first message and answered only from it; the assistant started from an empty context and could not see the sheet. The sheet is published next to the transcript with an audit of what the interview missed.

What the copilot did. In three rounds of questions it recovered the constraint the operator said "people forget": a fill site must run three validation batches before the filing, or wait six months for a supplement. It built one model with a version per option and checked that the operator's figures went in right: it reproduces their ~$250M of Kestrel first-year revenue and 54 batches a year at peak. Both follow from the inputs by construction, so this is a consistency check, not evidence that the model is right; that is what the plausibility checks are for. It then compared the options on paired paths, with readout outcomes forced and weighted by the stated odds.

What it found.

  • Reserve the slot now, usable by either product, and review it at Kestrel's readout: +$3.8M ± $1.1M against waiting at the portfolio committee's odds, and +$25M if a batch earns its peak-year value.
  • At the operator's own, lower odds for Kestrel (50%), it is break-even: +$1.6M ± $0.9M, just failing the brief's test. The recommendation is supply protection, not a bet on the odds.
  • If both succeed (27%), the reservation is worth about +$39M. Waiting leaves Tern short at launch on 26 of 37 paths and loses Tern demand on all 37, on average 11 batches worth $51M. In every other outcome the reservation is a bounded $9–11M cost.
  • A second line is $73–105M worse than waiting even when both succeed, confirming the operator's view.
  • A Tern-only reservation is safe only with a second CMO contract in writing. Without one it lost $72.5M when both succeed (3 paths, indicative), and the operator took it off the table.

The moment worth watching. The first pass came out at roughly break-even, because a rigid reservation carried a $72M loss on one path where only Tern succeeds. The model made that tail visible, and the operator supplied the fix: Kestrel reads out nine months earlier, so drop the reservation if Kestrel fails. The copilot encoded the rule, re-ran 30 paired paths and reported the result. The excerpt at the end of this page is that exchange, with the full session in a fold after it; it is also published as agent/sessions/launch_supply.html.

What the modeler sends on is the generated report, agent/studies/launch_supply/REPORT.pdf (18 pages): the answer and decision rule, the evidence, why a spreadsheet cannot produce it, the model in the operator's words with the one change that is the option, the execution map, and provenance down to the commit and all 208 stored runs. It is built from the study's files by agent/scripts/render_report.jl and never edited by hand.

8 · From the conversation to the model

The option the operator took to ExCo, reserve now and review at Kestrel's readout, is one JSON document: arm_cmo_exit.rdj.json. Because it is data, not code, every sentence the operator said can be traced to the element it became. The map below shows the whole model at the level of its parts, and the fold under it opens the same model element by element, drawn from the engine's own view of the net. The rows after it follow one quote at a time: what the operator said, the element as the document holds it, and what that element does. The document stores each expression as a tree of typed nodes; the rows print those trees as formulas so they can be read. In this study the documents were emitted by a generator script (gen_models.py) and each was then validated as data, so what is shown here is the validated document, not the script.

The same model, every element: 48 places, 31 transitions and 27 rules

In the screen version of this page: The same model, every element: 48 places, 31 transitions and 27 rules.

Operator, turn 3: “Effective is about 13 after changeovers and two 2-week shutdowns a year; the last 12 months averaged 12.8.”
place line · starts at 128

transition k_base_own
  takes    line [conserved] + cash×15
  gives    k_stock + own_used
  rate     r_k_base_own
  holds    1 month(s)
  succeeds 0.97
  priority 100

The line is a pool of 128 tenths of a batch per month: the measured 12.8, not the nominal 16. Every batch filled on it, here Kestrel's launch stock, takes one tenth for the month it runs and gives it back after (conserved). That is why Kestrel, Tern and the marketed products can collide on it. The cash×15 is the $150k variable cost per batch, charged in $1k units.

Operator, turn 3: “the fill site has to be in the BLA with three PPQ batches before we file”
rule t_file  (once)
  when  (((t_ok > 0.5) && (t >= ((14 + t_shift) + 3))) && !(t_filed > 0.5)) && (((cmo_now > 0.5) && (cmo_ppq > 0.5)) || (!(cmo_now > 0.5) && (t_ppq_done >= 30)))
  then  t_filed := 1
        t_file_t := t

rule t_cmo_pas  (once)
  when  ((!(cmo_now > 0.5) && (cmo_ppq > 0.5)) && (t_filed > 0.5)) && (t >= (max(cmo_ready_t, (t_file_t + 12)) + 6))
  then  t_cmo_ok := 1

The constraint the operator said people forget became a guard. Tern files only when its readout succeeded, three months have passed, and a fill site has its three PPQ batches, either at the CMO or on the own line (t_ppq_done >= 30 tenths). If the CMO qualifies after the filing, t_cmo_pas adds it six months later, the Prior Approval Supplement from the same answer.

Operator, turn 14: “I'd move Tern's PPQ in-house, file Tern on our own line, and give the CMO notice.”
rule cmo_exit_k  (once)
  when  (((cmo_now > 0.5) && (exit_at_k > 0.5)) && !(k_ok > 0.5)) && (t >= (5 + k_slip))
  then  cmo_active := 0
        cmo := 0
        cmo_ppq := 0
        cmo_now := 0
        cmo_exited := 1

param exit_at_k = 1    (the base model has 0: this is the option)

transition cmo_fee_y2
  takes    cash×5000
  gives    nothing (a pure cost)
  rate     1 * ((((cmo_now > 0.5) || (cmo_exited > 0.5)) && (t == 14)) && ((!(!(t_ok > 0.5) && (!(cmo_reassign > 0.5) || !(k_ok > 0.5))) && !(cmo_exited > 0.5)) || (walk2 > 0.5)))
  priority 1

The operator's rule, added in turn 15, is one rule and one switch. At Kestrel's readout (month 5 plus the drawn slip), if Kestrel failed, the reservation is dropped and Tern's PPQ falls back to the own line through the t_file guard above. The forfeited year's fee is still charged. exit_at_k is the only line that differs from the rigid reservation, arm_cmo.rdj.json. That is what the inertness check tested: on the same seeds, the two options came out identical in every world where Tern does not succeed alone.

7 more: what else the operator said, and where it went
Operator, turn 3: “Kestrel is 60% and Tern is 45%”
param p_k = 0.6

param p_t = 0.45

rule draws  (once)
  when  t >= 0
  then  k_ok := 1 * Bernoulli(p_k)
        t_ok := 1 * Bernoulli(p_t)
        k_slip := floor(Uniform(0, 3))
        t_shift := floor(Uniform(-3, 4))
        cmo_lead := floor(Uniform(12, 16))
        build_lead := floor(Uniform(30, 37))
        cmo2_lead := floor(Uniform(12, 16))
        k_success := k_ok
        t_success := t_ok

The odds are parameters. The readout outcomes and every lead time the operator gave as a range are drawn once, at the start, from the seeded stream. That is what lets the same seed give every option the same readout dates and lead times, the paired comparison the findings rest on.

Operator, turn 3: “about 3% of batches get rejected”
transition t_make_own
  takes    line [conserved] + cash×15
  gives    t_stock + own_used
  rate     r_t_make_own
  holds    1 month(s)
  succeeds 0.97
  priority 1

The reject rate is the succeeds 0.97 on every fill transition: a rejected batch still used its line time and its cost, but gives no stock.

Operator, turn 5: “Model it as competing for the same batches, not as free.”
transition t_ppq_own
  takes    line [conserved] + cash×15
  gives    t_ppq_done + own_used
  rate     r_ppq
  holds    1 month(s)
  succeeds 0.97
  priority 1

Tern's in-house PPQ is a fill transition like any other. It draws on the same line pool as Kestrel's launch stock, so the collision the operator predicted is something the engine produces, not an assumption written in.

Operator, turn 9: “I would never starve a launch to top up a buffer.”
transition k_base_own
  takes    line [conserved] + cash×15
  gives    k_stock + own_used
  rate     r_k_base_own
  holds    1 month(s)
  succeeds 0.97
  priority 100

transition k_buf_own
  takes    line [conserved] + cash×15
  gives    k_stock + own_used
  rate     r_k_buf_own
  holds    1 month(s)
  succeeds 0.97
  priority 0.5

param k_floor = 2

Kestrel's stock is split into two transitions over the same pool. The part up to the floor (k_floor, 2 months of forward stock) runs at priority 100, and the buffer above it at priority 0.5, below Tern's batches at 1. When the line is short, the buffer waits and the launch does not.

Operator, turn 3: “$5M a year for up to 4 batches a month”
transition cmo_fee
  takes    cash×5000
  gives    nothing (a pure cost)
  rate     1 * (((cmo_active > 0.5) && ((t >= cmo_start) && ((12 * floor(((t - cmo_start) / 12))) == (t - cmo_start)))) && !(((cmo_now > 0.5) || (cmo_exited > 0.5)) && (t == 14)))
  priority 1

rule cmo_ready  (once)
  when  ((cmo_active > 0.5) && !(cmo_resigned > 0.5)) && (t >= (cmo_start + cmo_lead))
  then  cmo := 40
        cmo_ppq := 1
        cmo_ready_t := t

The fee is a transition that takes 5000 ($5M in $1k units) from cash once a year while the contract is active. When the tech transfer finishes, cmo_ready fills the CMO pool with 40 tenths, the 4 batches a month.

Operator, turn 7: “Lost. In immunology an unsupplied patient goes to a competitor or stays on their current therapy.”
transition k_ship
  takes    k_orders + k_stock
  gives    k_sold
  rate     1000
  priority 10

transition k_lose
  takes    k_orders
  gives    k_lost
  rate     1000
  priority 0

An order that finds stock ships at priority 10 and earns the batch's value. One that does not is moved to k_lost the same month, never queued. That is the operator's answer, and also the engine's semantics: unmet demand is dropped, so it has to be counted explicitly.

Operator, turn 1: “Pay a CMO a reservation fee now to hold a fill-finish slot.”
param cmo_now = 1    (the base model has 0: this is the option)

rule cmo_sign_now  (once)
  when  (cmo_now > 0.5) && (t >= 1)
  then  cmo_active := 1
        cmo_start := 2

The option itself is a switch. cmo_now = 1 against the base model's 0 is the whole difference between reserving and waiting, apart from exit_at_k above. Every other element is shared, so a difference in the result can only come from the decision.

In the screen version of this page: the option the operator chose, as the engine receives it: reserve now, review at Kestrel's readout (agent/studies/launch_supply/arm_cmo_exit.rdj.json).

9 · What this does not show
  • That a real person answers like the role-played one. Both sides of the case were AI, and the operator was cooperative. It demonstrates the workflow, not anyone's supply plan.
  • Ensemble-grade evidence. Every comparison rests on paired single-seed paths, because a 96-seed ensemble took 53 minutes (W24).
  • That the soft inputs were tested. The interview recorded demand ±30%, marketed growth and readout correlation, then tested none of them. The published audit leads with them.
  • That the briefing's prose is checked. The numbers are generated from runs; the prose is written by the model. The assistant caught and retracted two of its own claims in this case; a critic that does this systematically is W9.
  • That every pattern is checked equally. Contention is checked end to end; feedback and mid-course decisions ran here without loop checks; coupled models do not yet go through the copilot (W25).
  • That the planned pieces exist. The outlined boxes in §5 are the direction, not shipped.
13 exchanges omitted Thirteen earlier turns: framing (the odds, the validation-batch rule, line capacity, the CMO terms), the model and its checks, a first report, and the operator's challenge, after which the assistant retracted two of its own claims and found the $72M loss on one Tern-only path.
The operator changes the rule
Operator

This is what I can use. The corrections on "on time" and the worst case are exactly what I'd have been caught on. I won't quote late-filing numbers at all until you've resolved the mismatch with the baseline.

But your Tern-only result doesn't match how I'd actually run the business. Kestrel reads out in March 2027, nine months before Tern. If Kestrel fails, our line suddenly has plenty of room. I wouldn't keep paying $0.4M a batch at the CMO and bet Tern's filing on their qualification schedule. I'd move Tern's PPQ in-house, file Tern on our own line, and give the CMO notice. That means eating the $5–10M. So the reservation isn't a fixed bet on Tern; it's something we decide to keep or drop at Kestrel's readout. Model it that way:

  • Kestrel fails: Tern's PPQ goes in-house, we give CMO notice, and we pay the forfeit.
  • Kestrel succeeds: Tern stays at the CMO, as you have it now.

My guess is that this cuts the Tern-only loss down to roughly the both-fail cost, and takes the $72M tail away. If I'm wrong, show me.

On your questions:

  1. Backup qualification. Regulatory will tell me it's feasible to list both sites in the BLA if both have PPQ data at filing. My estimate is about $0.5M a batch all-in including drug substance, so around $1.5M for three batches. The real cost, though, is three batches of line time right in the middle of Kestrel's stock build, which is the same collision you already found. So the backup only makes sense when Kestrel has failed. That's the rule above. Don't model the backup as a separate option in the both-succeed world unless it's cheap to do.
  1. Run budget. Spend it on the Tern-only outcome, with the exit-at-Kestrel-readout rule built in. The no-second-contract sensitivity can wait, because I'm taking the "Tern-only" structure off the table unless we have the second contract in writing.

If the rule change brings the Tern-only loss into line, I'll take this version to ExCo: reserve now, review at Kestrel's readout. Send me that result and the final one-paragraph wording, and we're done.

Claude

Phase 4 of 4, the final result. Your guess holds: under the review rule, the Tern-only outcome costs $9.4M against waiting, and the $72M tail is gone.

The rule: reserve the CMO now and review at Kestrel's readout. If Kestrel fails, Tern's validation batches move in-house, Tern files on our own line, we give the CMO notice and pay the forfeit. If Kestrel succeeds, nothing changes.

  • Tern-only outcome, 30 paired paths: −$9.4M ± $0.02M against waiting. Every path falls between −$9.7M and −$9.3M.
  • It is pure cost. Tern's revenue is the same as under waiting on all 30 paths, Tern files on time on all 30, and no Tern demand is lost on any of them.
  • The old rule (keep the CMO no matter what) lost $31.7M ± $6.0M in this outcome over 10 paths. Four of those 10 lost more than $20M, the worst lost $72.1M, and 4 of 10 filed late.
  • Every outcome except both-succeed now costs about the same:
OutcomeCost vs waitingPaths
Kestrel only$8.8M3
Tern only$9.4M30
Both fail$9.4M3

The 3-path outcomes vary by less than $0.1M across their paths.

  • Expected value: +$3.8M ± $1.1M against waiting (plausible range +$1.6M to +$6.0M). This now passes the brief's test that the value minus two standard errors stays above zero. Last turn it failed, at −$1.1M ± $3.4M.
  • At the peak value per batch: +$25.0M ± $2.8M.
  • If Kestrel's odds of success are 0.50: +$1.6M ± $0.9M, which just misses the test (−$0.2M at the low end).
  • What the review rule does not change: when both succeed, the rule never fires. The value there stays at +$38.7M ± $4.1M over 37 paths, and Tern's launch is fully supplied on all 37 (under waiting, Tern is short on 26 of 37). The remaining downside is also in that outcome: 3 of 37 paths lose money, the worst $14.3M. That happens when Tern reads out before the CMO has qualified and has to wait for it.

The late-filing mismatch is resolved. It was sampling chance, not a model error.

  • I re-ran three members of the 96-seed baseline with their exact seeds. They reproduce its late-filing months and lost Tern demand exactly, with or without the outcomes forced.
  • The value gap is a constant 0.72% from a one-month discounting convention, which cancels out of every comparison.
  • Our 13 late filings in 37 paths against the baseline's 2 in 20 fits chance (p = 0.06). Pooled, the rate is 15 in 57 (range 0.17–0.39). The two sources do differ on how long a late filing runs: 2–3 months in our paths, 2 months in both of the baseline's cases.
  • Late filing is about as likely either way: 0.10 overall under reserve-and-review, 0.095 under waiting.
  • The figures are usable now, but quote them with their range: they are the least precise numbers in the report.

Backup qualification: I did not model it at your $0.5M a batch. The model charges $0.15M a batch for in-house validation, so its cost in the Tern-only outcome is understated by about $1M. That is arithmetic, not a simulated result.

One paragraph for ExCo:

We recommend reserving the CMO fill-finish slot now, before 30 November 2026, usable by either Kestrel or Tern, and reviewing it at Kestrel's readout in March 2027. If Kestrel fails, Tern's validation batches move to our own line, Tern files there, and we give the CMO notice and pay the forfeit. If Kestrel succeeds, the reservation stands. Modelled over paired simulated paths, this is worth about +$3.8M (±$1.1M) against waiting at the stated odds, and about +$25M if a batch earns its peak-year value. Its purpose is supply protection. If both assets succeed (27% chance), it is worth about +$39M and removes a Tern launch shortfall that otherwise occurs on 26 of 37 paths, costing on average about 11 batches and $51M. If either asset fails, the cost is a bounded $9–10M: over 30 paths in the Tern-only case, and 3 each for Kestrel-only and both-fail. We do not recommend building a second line: it is about $84–87M worse than waiting even when both succeed (8 paths). We do not recommend a Tern-only reservation unless the second CMO contract is in writing. The one remaining risk is a late CMO qualification delaying Tern's filing if both succeed: 3 of 37 paths lose money, the worst about $14M.

Phase 1 — FRAME
Operator

I need a decision on fill-finish capacity for executive committee in about five weeks, and I want it to hold up when our CFO pushes back. Two Phase-3 biologics are coming. Kestrel reads out around March next year, and Tern reads out around the end of 2027. We have one sterile fill-finish line in-house, and most of it is already taken by our marketed products. It can probably carry one launch but not two. There are three options on the table:

  1. Pay a CMO a reservation fee now to hold a fill-finish slot.
  2. Build a second line of our own.
  3. Wait for the readouts and decide then.

My problem is lead time: tech transfer, PPQ and the build all take longer than the gap between readout and launch. Our CFO says we shouldn't pay for capacity on an asset that has less than even odds. What I need from you is a recommendation I can defend, what it costs, what it protects us against, and what would change the answer. I don't care about the modeling details. I care about whether we launch with product on the shelf.

Claude

Phase 1 of 4: framing the decision. I'll need about three rounds of questions from you, then I build the model, check that it behaves sensibly, and bring you a recommendation.

Is simulation worth it here? Yes. All three tests pass:

  • Capacity is tight. One sterile line, mostly taken by marketed products. It can carry one launch, not two.
  • Things take time. Tech transfer, PPQ and a new build all take longer than the gap between readout and launch. That is exactly why "wait and decide" is risky.
  • A choice gets forced along the way. If both assets succeed, and their timing overlaps, someone has to decide who gets the in-house line and who goes short.

Your CFO's argument ("don't pay for capacity on an asset with less than even odds") treats each asset on its own. The real risk sits in the scenario where both succeed and collide on one line. A per-asset spreadsheet can't price that. A simulation that plays out the readouts, the lead times and the line allocation thousands of times can. It will also tell you if the CFO is right, and if so I'll say so.

First round. Rough numbers are fine, and a range is better than a guess:

  1. Odds and value. What probability of success do you put on Kestrel and on Tern, and which one is under 50%? What is a month of lost or delayed launch worth for each: first-year sales, peak sales, or a per-month figure? And what units do you use (for example $M, months)?
  2. Lead times from the day you commit. How long until usable capacity for (a) a CMO slot, meaning reservation, tech transfer and PPQ, and (b) a second in-house line, meaning build, qualification and PPQ? And how long from readout to planned launch for each asset? My working assumption: a CMO is faster but not fast enough, and a build is slower still. Correct me.
  3. The cheques. What are the CMO reservation fee, the build cost, and any running costs? Do you lose the fee if the asset fails, or can you cancel or reassign the slot to the other asset? That last point matters a lot for your CFO's argument.

After that I'll have a smaller second round: how the in-house line gets shared if both succeed, and what result would change your mind.

Phase 2 — AUTHOR · Phase 3 — DIAGNOSE
Claude

AUTHOR (phase 2 of 4) is done. The model is built, checked and matched to your numbers.

In your "both succeed" world with no reservation, we launch Kestrel with product on the shelf and Tern is the one that launches short. Tern ends up on the losing side of your priority rule. One sample path shows how:

  • Tern's qualification batches wait behind Kestrel's launch-stock build on our line, so Tern files 2 months late.
  • In Tern's first 12 months it gets about 5 batches against the roughly 20 it needs.
  • Kestrel loses nothing.

That is one path, not a forecast; the probabilities come from the full run. The same pattern shows up everywhere I tested. Kestrel only ever ran short in one setup (a Tern-only CMO slot with both assets succeeding), and even there not in its first six months. So the 15% share-loss penalty may almost never trigger. If that holds, the case for the CMO is about protecting Tern, not Kestrel.

What is in the model, in your terms:

  • Wait now takes the mid-2028 CMO opening if either readout succeeds, as you asked.
  • Reserve now makes the CMO Tern's filing site from day one. Kestrel can use the slot too (via the prior-approval supplement) if Tern fails. If both assets fail, we walk away for $10M, with $5M as a sensitivity.
  • Tern-only reservation: if Tern fails, it now falls back to the mid-2028 opening for Kestrel. Without that it would have been a straw man.
  • Second line at 12 batches/month, capex phased 20/50/30 (even spread as a check). Two versions: approved now, or after Tern's readout if both succeed.
  • Base case values batches at the year-1 rate, with the peak rate as the upside test. Lost sales are gone, not delayed. Kestrel is also re-tested at 50% odds.
  • Calibration: Kestrel's first 12 months come out at $250.0M and 54 batches a year by the end. Tern's first 12 months come out at $151.1M. You gave $250M, 54 and $150M.

I also fixed two bugs found while checking: the first-year CMO fee and the first month of capex were not being charged.

Two questions before I run the full set of scenarios:

  1. Who waits when the line is full? Your rule is marketed, then Kestrel, then Tern. Taken literally, Kestrel keeps a full six months of stock on the shelf before Tern gets a single batch.
  • The alternative: Kestrel's launch stock and this month's demand come first, then Tern, then Kestrel's buffer top-up.
  • On the same sample path, the alternative gives Tern about 14 batches in its first year instead of 5, and Kestrel still loses nothing.
  • Proposed default: your literal rule as the base case, with the alternative shown next to it, because ExCo may ask why Kestrel's buffer beats Tern's launch.
  1. Could we hold two CMO contracts? If we reserve the slot for Tern only and both readouts succeed, could Kestrel also take the mid-2028 opening as a second contract?
  • Proposed default: no. One reservation, and the mid-2028 opening only serves as the fallback if the first contract ends.

With those two answers I'll run all options with the same readout outcomes in every option, so the differences between options are like-for-like. Then comes phase 3, the numbers with error bars.

Everything is in agent/studies/launch_supply/: BRIEF.md (updated with your turn-4 decisions), DOCTOR.md (the checks), capture_log.md (turn 4 logged) and gen_models.py, which writes the 11 scenario files. All of it is committed.

agent/studies/launch_supply/gen_models.pyagent/studies/launch_supply/DOCTOR.mdagent/studies/launch_supply/BRIEF.md (turn-4 decisions)
Operator

That sample path is useful, but it also shows my rule was written wrong. Here's what I actually meant.

  1. Who waits. Use the alternative as the base case, and show the literal rule next to it. The priority I gave you was about supplying patients, not about letting Kestrel sit on a full six months of stock while Tern launches empty. Six months is a launch policy; I wouldn't hold that much forever. I would never starve a launch to top up a buffer. Apply one guardrail in both versions: Kestrel never goes below about 2 months of forward stock. That floor is my estimate of what I'd actually defend to Quality and commercial. If it complicates the model, drop it, but tell me whether it changes anything.
  1. Two CMO contracts. Yes, allow it. If both assets succeed, I'm not going to leave a $1.2B product without capacity to save $5M a year. I'd sign a second contract the same week. Assume the same terms as the first ($5M a year, 3-year minimum, $0.4M per batch, 12–15 months to ready). That's my estimate, since I don't have a second term sheet. Show the no-second-contract case as a sensitivity. If "reserve for Tern now, add a second slot later" beats "reserve now for both", that's a real finding and I want it clearly stated.

Two things I want in the results:

  • Kestrel's short case. Flag the one setup where Kestrel runs short, even if it's outside the first six months. The first thing ExCo will ask is whether Kestrel is ever at risk.
  • Tern's late filing. Give me the odds that Tern files late because its PPQ is stuck behind Kestrel, under each option. A two-month slip in filing is two months off Tern's launch, and that's something the CFO understands without a model.

Run it.

Phase 4 — REPORT
Claude

Phase 4 of 4 (Report). My recommendation is to sign the CMO reservation before 30 November, with the slot usable for either asset. Don't build a second line, now or after the readouts. Only tie the slot to Tern alone if you can get the second CMO contract in writing.

How the numbers were made. Ensembles were cancelled, so none of these figures is an ensemble average. Each option was simulated on a few example paths in each of the four readout outcomes (both succeed, Kestrel only, Tern only, neither). There are 3 paths per option when both succeed and 1 path in each other outcome, with the same paths for every option. The four outcomes were then weighted by your odds (Kestrel 0.60, Tern 0.45). I measured no spread, so no figure has an error bar. Where the paths disagree, I say so.

Money: the CFO is right. Reserving now is worth +$0.9M against waiting ($847.9M vs $847.0M), which is a tie. Taking the three both-succeed paths one at a time, the gain is +$5.7M, −$12.2M or +$9.3M, so even its sign is not settled.

What the fee buys: Tern's launch.

  • The chance of a supply-short Tern launch drops from 0.18 to 0.00.
  • The chance of Tern filing late because its PPQ is stuck behind Kestrel drops from 0.18 (2 months late) to 0.00.
  • If both readouts fail, the reservation costs $9.4M.

The decision for ExCo is whether to pay up to about $9M of downside to take Tern's shortage risk from about 1 in 5 to zero.

OptioneNPV vs wait ($M)Chance Tern short at launchChance Tern files lateIf both fail ($M)
Wait—0.180.180.0
Reserve now, both assets+0.90.000.09 *−9.4
Reserve now for Tern, second slot later+0.30.000.09 *−9.4
Build after the readouts−23.00.180.180.0
Build now−105.80.180.18−123.9

\* This remaining late filing (3 months) is not caused by Kestrel. On one path Tern read out early and the CMO was not yet qualified.

Your turn-5 questions:

  • Kestrel at launch: it is never short in its first six months, under any option or sensitivity.
  • Is Kestrel ever at risk? Only in one setup, which I've flagged: slot reserved for Tern and no second contract. There Kestrel runs short on every both-succeed path. It loses about 102–107 batches of demand, all after its first six months. The weighted chance is 0.27.
  • Does "reserve for Tern now, add a second slot later" beat "reserve for both"? No. With the second contract it ties (+$0.3M vs +$0.9M). Without it, it is the worst CMO option: −$72.5M, while reserve-for-both is +$16.3M.
  • Check before signing: can Kestrel move into the reserved slot if Tern fails? That is your reading of the term sheet, not confirmed.

What would change the answer (reserve-now for both, against waiting):

  • Stronger: valuing batches at the peak rate gives +$20.4M. With no second contract it is +$16.3M, and +$61.1M at peak value. Your literal allocation rule gives +$8.8M.
  • Weaker: at Kestrel PoS 0.50 it ties at −$1.5M, and Tern's protection is unchanged.
  • Small effects: a $5M walk-away instead of $10M adds about +$1.0M. Spreading build capex evenly costs $0.95M more. The 2-month Kestrel floor made no difference.
  • Building comes out negative in every case I ran.

Caveats:

  • Each outcome other than "both succeed" rests on one path.
  • Everything stops at end-2032, so the build options are understated by whatever the line earns after that.
  • The completed 96-seed baseline ensemble was sent as a file I'm not allowed to read, so I did not use it. Paste its result into the conversation if you want it as a cross-check.

Possible next steps: get the second contract's terms in writing (this decides between the two reservation structures); rerun the single-asset outcomes on two more seeds each (12 runs, about 8 minutes); test whether letting Tern's PPQ fall back to our own line removes the last 3-month delay.

Editorial note — the run budget changed mid-session. Phase 4 was first planned as 96-seed ensembles per option. The first one took 53 minutes on this machine, because rdx ensemble rebuilds and recompiles the model for every seed (tracked as W24). The operator cancelled the batch after the baseline finished and set the budget to single-seed runs only. So every comparison below is a set of paired example paths — the same seed gives every option the same readout dates and lead times — with readout outcomes forced and weighted by the stated odds, not an ensemble. The one finished 96-seed ensemble (wait only) was later passed to the assistant as a cross-check.

Operator

That's clearer than I expected, and the headline I'd take into the room is honest: reserving is roughly free in expectation, and it's insurance for Tern's launch. But before I stand up in front of ExCo with it, three things.

  1. A "0.18" chance isn't a measured probability. If I have this right, both succeeding is 0.60 × 0.45 = 0.27, and Tern ran short on 2 of your 3 both-succeed paths. So 0.18 is 0.27 × 2/3. That's "2 runs out of 3", not "1 in 5". The CFO will take that apart in about ten seconds. I need more than three paths on the both-succeed case, which is the only case where any of this matters. Run your 12 extra runs, and add enough both-succeed paths to put a range on these numbers, however long that takes. I'd rather wait a day than present two runs out of three.
  1. What does "Tern short" cost? You say the fee takes Tern's shortage risk to zero, but the value of that protection comes out close to zero. If a short Tern launch cost a lot, reserving would be worth a lot more than +$0.9M. So either the shortfall is small, or the lost batches are cheap at the year-1 rate. Tell me how many batches and how many $M Tern loses on a short path, and how long it's short. And the −$12.2M path: what happened there? If one of three paths loses money, I need to know why.
  1. Framing for ExCo. Check my version. "Sign the CMO reservation before 30 November, usable for either product. Expected value is neutral, around break-even. Worst case, if both trials fail, we lose about $9–10M. In return, if both succeed, Tern launches on time and in full instead of short and two months late. Building a second line loses money in every case we ran." Is anything in that overstated?

On my side, I'll get legal to confirm that Kestrel can use the slot if Tern fails. I'll also ask the CMO for indicative terms on a second slot. Keep the "reserve for Tern only" structure off the recommendation unless we have that second contract in writing, and I agree with that.

One more request: the 96-seed baseline you mentioned. I don't have it and can't paste it. If it exists on your side, get it used properly, or rerun it. A 96-path baseline is exactly the error bar I'm asking for.

Claude

Phase 4 of 4 (Report), revised. I made 90 more runs this turn. The both-succeed case now rests on 37 paths for wait and reserve-now, and you get the ranges you asked for. The recommendation stands: reserve the CMO slot now, usable by either product, and don't build. More paths did change two things I told you last turn.

What changed from last turn

  • Expected value moved from +$0.9M to −$1.1M against waiting, with a standard error of $3.4M across paths. That puts it between about −$8M and +$6M. It is still neutral. Most of that uncertainty comes from the Tern-only world, which has only 3 paths.
  • Correction: reserving does not make a late Tern filing less likely. It changes the cause. When both succeed, Tern files late on 14 of 37 paths if we reserve (1–3 months) and on 13 of 37 if we wait (2–3 months). Counting all four outcomes, the chance of a late filing is 0.16 with the reservation and 0.10 without. Last turn's "0.18 to 0.09" came from three paths, and I withdraw it.

1. The ranges. When both succeed, Tern is short at launch on 26 of 37 wait paths. That is 0.70, with a 95% range of 0.54–0.83. Multiplied by the 0.27 chance that both succeed, it gives 0.19 overall (range 0.15–0.22). With the reservation, Tern is short on 0 of 37 paths (at most 0.09 given both succeed, so at most 0.025 overall).

The 96-seed baseline you couldn't paste was relayed to me directly, so I have now used it. It covers the wait option only, so it cannot put an error bar on the difference between options, but it does check the wait figures:

Measure (wait)Our paths predict96-seed baseline
eNPV$751M$766M ± $62M
Chance Tern short at launch0.1350.146 ± 0.036
Chance Kestrel short at some point0.0210.021
Tern demand lost20.6 tenths of a batch24.0 ± 5.0 tenths
Late filing, average months per path0.1450.042 ± 0.029

The first four agree. For the comparison I reweighted our paths to the success rates the baseline actually drew, because its random draws came out below your odds (Kestrel 0.54, Tern 0.35). The late-filing row does not agree: our paths show roughly three times more late filing than the baseline, and I can't yet say why. Resolving it needs the baseline's per-seed results. Until then, treat the late-filing numbers as the least reliable ones here.

2. What "Tern short" costs. When both succeed and we wait, Tern loses demand on every path, 37 of 37, not only on the 26 that count as short at launch. On average it loses 10.7 batches, worth $50.9M in present value (range 5.9–20.3 batches, $27.8–95.9M). The shortfall lasts 5 to 10 months and starts right after Tern's launch, around late 2029.

Net of fees, the reservation is worth +$38.7M ± $4.1M when both succeed, and it comes out ahead on 34 of 37 paths. The protection is real, but it is only needed in 27% of outcomes. In the other outcomes the reservation costs money:

OutcomeChanceReserve now vs wait
Both succeed0.27+$38.7M
Kestrel only0.33−$8.8M
Tern only0.18−$18.6M to −$72.1M
Both fail0.22−$9.4M

That is why the expected value nets to about zero. The value of the protection depends on what a Tern batch is worth. At the peak rate it is +$117.3M when both succeed and +$15.0M ± $8.8M overall. At Kestrel PoS 0.50 it is −$4.5M ± $4.1M.

The path that loses money. Last turn's −$12.2M path is seed 2; it is −$14.3M in the both-succeed world. Tern read out early. Under wait, Tern ran its qualification batches on our own line and launched in month 30. Under the reservation, Tern is filed with the CMO, and the CMO was not qualified in time, so filing slipped 3 months and Tern launched in month 33. Three months of lost early sales cancelled out the shortage we avoided, and the fees made the path negative. All 3 of the 37 losing paths work this way.

The same thing drives the Tern-only outcome, where one path costs $72.1M. Making the CMO Tern's only filed site creates a new risk, and it is what keeps this option from paying. A possible fix is to keep a backup qualification on our own line. I can model that if it's regulatorily feasible (question below).

3. Your framing. Two sentences need changing.

  • "Expected value is neutral, around break-even": correct.
  • "Worst case, if both trials fail, we lose about $9–10M": both failing costs $9.4M, but that is not the worst case. If only Tern succeeds, the reservation cost $18.6–19.5M on 2 of 3 paths and $72.1M on the path where the CMO was late.
  • "If both succeed, Tern launches on time and in full instead of short and two months late": "in full" holds (0 of 37 short vs 26 of 37); "on time" does not. Late filings are about as frequent either way; they just come from a different source.
  • "Building a second line loses money in every case we ran": correct. All 8 both-succeed paths are negative (−$72.8M to −$104.7M for building now), as is every other path. Add "within the 2032 horizon", since value after 2032 isn't counted.

A version you could say: "Sign the reservation before 30 November, usable for either product. In expectation it is roughly neutral, within a few million dollars either way. It is insurance. If both products succeed, Tern launches fully supplied: in our model it is otherwise short about 7 times in 10, losing around $50M of sales. The cost is about $9M if both fail, about $19M if only Tern succeeds, and more if the CMO qualifies late on a Tern-only success. Building a second line loses money in every case we ran through 2032."

Kestrel: it is never short at launch (0 of 37 paths either way). Under wait it runs slightly short later in life on 4 of 37 paths, losing 0.5–3.7 batches; it never does with the reservation.

Still open:

  • The literal-rule and no-second-contract sensitivities still rest on 3 paths from last turn. Rerunning them on 37 paths would take about 4 × 34 runs, which is more than today's run budget allowed.
  • The Tern-only outcome has 3 paths and drives most of the uncertainty in the expected value.

Two questions:

  1. Could Tern be filed with the CMO while also qualifying on our own line as a backup, filing with whichever site is ready first? If so, what would it cost? I'd model it with 3 extra qualification batches on our line. That targets the one risk the reservation creates.
  2. Should the next run budget go on the Tern-only outcome (about 30 paths, roughly 30 minutes) or on the no-second-contract sensitivity? I'd put it on Tern-only, because it decides whether "neutral" is closer to −$8M or +$6M.

Operator

This is what I can use. The corrections on "on time" and the worst case are exactly what I'd have been caught on. I won't quote late-filing numbers at all until you've resolved the mismatch with the baseline.

But your Tern-only result doesn't match how I'd actually run the business. Kestrel reads out in March 2027, nine months before Tern. If Kestrel fails, our line suddenly has plenty of room. I wouldn't keep paying $0.4M a batch at the CMO and bet Tern's filing on their qualification schedule. I'd move Tern's PPQ in-house, file Tern on our own line, and give the CMO notice. That means eating the $5–10M. So the reservation isn't a fixed bet on Tern; it's something we decide to keep or drop at Kestrel's readout. Model it that way:

  • Kestrel fails: Tern's PPQ goes in-house, we give CMO notice, and we pay the forfeit.
  • Kestrel succeeds: Tern stays at the CMO, as you have it now.

My guess is that this cuts the Tern-only loss down to roughly the both-fail cost, and takes the $72M tail away. If I'm wrong, show me.

On your questions:

  1. Backup qualification. Regulatory will tell me it's feasible to list both sites in the BLA if both have PPQ data at filing. My estimate is about $0.5M a batch all-in including drug substance, so around $1.5M for three batches. The real cost, though, is three batches of line time right in the middle of Kestrel's stock build, which is the same collision you already found. So the backup only makes sense when Kestrel has failed. That's the rule above. Don't model the backup as a separate option in the both-succeed world unless it's cheap to do.
  1. Run budget. Spend it on the Tern-only outcome, with the exit-at-Kestrel-readout rule built in. The no-second-contract sensitivity can wait, because I'm taking the "Tern-only" structure off the table unless we have the second contract in writing.

If the rule change brings the Tern-only loss into line, I'll take this version to ExCo: reserve now, review at Kestrel's readout. Send me that result and the final one-paragraph wording, and we're done.

Claude

Phase 4 of 4, the final result. Your guess holds: under the review rule, the Tern-only outcome costs $9.4M against waiting, and the $72M tail is gone.

The rule: reserve the CMO now and review at Kestrel's readout. If Kestrel fails, Tern's validation batches move in-house, Tern files on our own line, we give the CMO notice and pay the forfeit. If Kestrel succeeds, nothing changes.

  • Tern-only outcome, 30 paired paths: −$9.4M ± $0.02M against waiting. Every path falls between −$9.7M and −$9.3M.
  • It is pure cost. Tern's revenue is the same as under waiting on all 30 paths, Tern files on time on all 30, and no Tern demand is lost on any of them.
  • The old rule (keep the CMO no matter what) lost $31.7M ± $6.0M in this outcome over 10 paths. Four of those 10 lost more than $20M, the worst lost $72.1M, and 4 of 10 filed late.
  • Every outcome except both-succeed now costs about the same:
OutcomeCost vs waitingPaths
Kestrel only$8.8M3
Tern only$9.4M30
Both fail$9.4M3

The 3-path outcomes vary by less than $0.1M across their paths.

  • Expected value: +$3.8M ± $1.1M against waiting (plausible range +$1.6M to +$6.0M). This now passes the brief's test that the value minus two standard errors stays above zero. Last turn it failed, at −$1.1M ± $3.4M.
  • At the peak value per batch: +$25.0M ± $2.8M.
  • If Kestrel's odds of success are 0.50: +$1.6M ± $0.9M, which just misses the test (−$0.2M at the low end).
  • What the review rule does not change: when both succeed, the rule never fires. The value there stays at +$38.7M ± $4.1M over 37 paths, and Tern's launch is fully supplied on all 37 (under waiting, Tern is short on 26 of 37). The remaining downside is also in that outcome: 3 of 37 paths lose money, the worst $14.3M. That happens when Tern reads out before the CMO has qualified and has to wait for it.

The late-filing mismatch is resolved. It was sampling chance, not a model error.

  • I re-ran three members of the 96-seed baseline with their exact seeds. They reproduce its late-filing months and lost Tern demand exactly, with or without the outcomes forced.
  • The value gap is a constant 0.72% from a one-month discounting convention, which cancels out of every comparison.
  • Our 13 late filings in 37 paths against the baseline's 2 in 20 fits chance (p = 0.06). Pooled, the rate is 15 in 57 (range 0.17–0.39). The two sources do differ on how long a late filing runs: 2–3 months in our paths, 2 months in both of the baseline's cases.
  • Late filing is about as likely either way: 0.10 overall under reserve-and-review, 0.095 under waiting.
  • The figures are usable now, but quote them with their range: they are the least precise numbers in the report.

Backup qualification: I did not model it at your $0.5M a batch. The model charges $0.15M a batch for in-house validation, so its cost in the Tern-only outcome is understated by about $1M. That is arithmetic, not a simulated result.

One paragraph for ExCo:

We recommend reserving the CMO fill-finish slot now, before 30 November 2026, usable by either Kestrel or Tern, and reviewing it at Kestrel's readout in March 2027. If Kestrel fails, Tern's validation batches move to our own line, Tern files there, and we give the CMO notice and pay the forfeit. If Kestrel succeeds, the reservation stands. Modelled over paired simulated paths, this is worth about +$3.8M (±$1.1M) against waiting at the stated odds, and about +$25M if a batch earns its peak-year value. Its purpose is supply protection. If both assets succeed (27% chance), it is worth about +$39M and removes a Tern launch shortfall that otherwise occurs on 26 of 37 paths, costing on average about 11 batches and $51M. If either asset fails, the cost is a bounded $9–10M: over 30 paths in the Tern-only case, and 3 each for Kestrel-only and both-fail. We do not recommend building a second line: it is about $84–87M worse than waiting even when both succeed (8 paths). We do not recommend a Tern-only reservation unless the second CMO contract is in writing. The one remaining risk is a late CMO qualification delaying Tern's filing if both succeed: 3 of 37 paths lose money, the worst about $14M.

I checked all three edits against the runs, and all three are in the paragraph below. The build range needed one fix.

  1. Kestrel at 50%: correct. At 50% the reservation comes to +$1.6M ± $0.9M against waiting. The low end of the range is slightly below zero (−$0.2M), so "roughly break-even" is fair.
  2. Build numbers: your −$73M to −$105M is the range for building now only. Building after the readouts has its own, narrower range: −$82M to −$87M. Both are over 8 paths where both products succeed, and the paragraph now gives both ranges.
